extracted-character range 0:16000]\n--- PAGE 1 ---\nAgentic Context Engineering: Evolving Contexts for Self-Improving\nLanguage Models\nQizheng Zhang 1∗\nChangran Hu 2∗\nShubhangi Upasani 2\nBoyuan Ma 2\nFenglu Hong 2\nVamsidhar Kamanuru 2\nJay Rainton 2\nChen Wu 2\nMengmeng Ji 2\nHanchen Li 3\nUrmish Thakker 2\nJames Zou 1\nKunle Olukotun 1\n1 Stanford University\n2 SambaNova Systems, Inc.\n3 UC Berkeley\n∗equal contribution\n# qizhengz@stanford.edu, changran.hu@sambanovasystems.com\nAbstract\nLarge language model (LLM) applications such as agents and domain-specific reasoning increasingly rely on\ncontext adaptation—modifying inputs with instructions, strategies, or evidence, rather than weight updates.\nPrior approaches improve usability but often suffer from brevity bias, which drops domain insights for\nconcise summaries, and from context collapse, where iterative rewriting erodes details over time. Building on\nthe adaptive memory introduced by Dynamic Cheatsheet, we introduce ACE (Agentic Context Engineering),\na framework that treats contexts as evolving playbooks that accumulate, refine, and organize strategies\nthrough a modular process of generation, reflection, and curation. ACE prevents collapse with structured,\nincremental updates that preserve detailed knowledge and scale with long-context models. Across agent\nand domain-specific benchmarks, ACE optimizes contexts both offline (e.g., system prompts) and online (e.g.,\nagent memory), consistently outperforming strong baselines: +10.6% on agents and +8.6% on finance, while\nsignificantly reducing adaptation latency and rollout cost. Notably, ACE could adapt effectively without\nlabeled supervision and instead by leveraging natural execution feedback. On the AppWorld leaderboard,\nACE matches the top-ranked production-level agent on the overall average and surpasses it on the harder\ntest-challenge split, despite using a smaller open-source model. These results show that comprehensive,\nevolving contexts enable scalable, efficient, and self-improving LLM systems with low overhead.\n1\nIntroduction\nBase LLM\nICL\nGEPA\nDC\nACE\n40.0\n42.5\n45.0\n47.5\n50.0\n52.5\n55.0\n57.5\n60.0\nAccuracy (%)\n42.4%\n46.0% 46.4%\n51.9%\n59.5%\nAgent: AppWorld\nBase LLM\nICL\nGEPA\nDC\nACE\n68\n70\n72\n74\n76\n78\n80\n82\n70.7%\n72.3%\n73.5%\n74.2%\n78.3%\nDomain Knowledge: FiNER\nBase LLM\nICL\nGEPA\nDC\nACE\n66\n68\n70\n72\n74\n76\n78\n80\n67.5% 67.0%\n71.5%\n69.5%\n76.5%\nNumerical Reasoning: Formula\nFigure 1: Overall Performance Results. Our proposed framework, ACE, consistently outperforms strong\nbaselines across agent and domain-specific reasoning tasks.\nModern AI applications based on large language models (LLMs), such as LLM agents [49, 52] and compound\nAI systems [55], increasingly depend on context adaptation. Instead of modifying model weights, context\narXiv:2510.04618v1  [cs.LG]  6 Oct 2025\n\n\n--- PAGE 2 ---\nadaptation improves performance after model training by incorporating clarified instructions, structured\nreasoning steps, or domain-specific input formats directly into the model’s inputs. Contexts underpin\nmany AI system components, including system prompts that guide downstream tasks [4, 36], memory that\ncarries past facts and experiences [41, 48], and factual evidence that reduces hallucination and supplements\nknowledge [6].\nAdapting through contexts rather than weights offers several key advantages. Contexts are interpretable and\nexplainable for users and developers [45, 47], allow rapid integration of new knowledge at runtime [7, 27],\nand can be shared across models or modules in a compound system [23]. Meanwhile, advances in long-\ncontext LLMs [39] and context-efficient inference such as KV cache reuse [17, 51] are making context-based\napproaches increasingly practical for deployment. As a result, context adaptation is emerging as a central\nparadigm for building capable, scalable, and self-improving AI systems.\nDespite this progress, existing approaches to context adaptation face two key limitations. First, a brevity\nbias: many prompt optimizers prioritize concise, broadly applicable instructions over comprehensive\naccumulation. For example, GEPA [4] highlights brevity as a strength, but such abstraction can omit\ndomain-specific heuristics, tool-use guidelines, or common failure modes that matter in practice [16]. This\nobjective aligns with validation metrics in some settings, but often fails to capture the detailed strategies\nrequired by agents and knowledge-intensive applications. Second, context collapse: methods that rely on\nmonolithic rewriting by an LLM often degrade into shorter, less informative summaries over time, causing\nsharp performance declines (Figure 2). In domains such as interactive agents [38, 43, 57], domain-specific\nprogramming [53, 56], and financial or legal analysis [18, 33, 44], strong performance depends on retaining\ndetailed, task-specific knowledge rather than compressing it away.\nAs applications such as agents and knowledge-intensive reasoning demand greater reliability, recent work\nhas shifted toward saturating contexts with abundant, potentially useful information [11, 12, 22], enabled by\nadvances in long-context LLMs [34, 39]. We argue that contexts should function not as concise summaries,\nbut as comprehensive, evolving playbooks—detailed, inclusive, and rich with domain insights. Unlike\nhumans, who often benefit from concise generalization, LLMs are more effective when provided with long,\ndetailed contexts and can distill relevance autonomously [22, 31, 41]. Thus, instead of compressing away\ndomain-specific heuristics and tactics, contexts should preserve them, allowing the model to decide what\nmatters at inference time.\nTo address these limitations, we introduce ACE (Agentic Context Engineering), a framework for compre-\nhensive context adaptation in both offline settings (e.g., system prompt optimization) and online settings\n(e.g., test-time memory adaptation). Rather than compressing contexts into distilled summaries, ACE treats\nthem as evolving playbooks that accumulate and organize strategies over time. Building on the agentic\narchitecture of Dynamic Cheatsheet [41], ACE incorporates a modular workflow of generation, reflection,\nand curation, while adding structured, incremental updates guided by a grow-and-refine principle. This\ndesign preserves detailed, domain-specific knowledge, prevents context collapse, and yields contexts that\nremain comprehensive and scalable throughout adaptation.\nWe evaluate ACE on two categories of LLM applications that most benefit from comprehensive, evolving\ncontexts: (1) agents [43], which require multi-turn reasoning, tool use, and environment interaction, where\naccumulated strategies can be reused across episodes; and (2) domain-specific benchmarks, which demand\nspecialized tactics and knowledge, where we focus on financial analysis [33, 44]. Our key findings are:\n• ACE consistently outperforms strong baselines, yielding average gains of 10.6% on agents and 8.6% on\ndomain-specific benchmarks, across both offline and online adaptation settings.\n• ACE is able to construct effective contexts without labeled supervision, instead leveraging execution\nfeedback and environment signals—key ingredients for self-improving LLMs and agents.\n• On the AppWorld benchmark leaderboard [5], ACE matches the top-ranked production-level agent IBM-\nCUGA [35] (powered by GPT-4.1) on average and surpasses it on the harder test-challenge split, while\nusing a smaller open-source model (DeepSeek-V3.1).\n2\n\n\n--- PAGE 3 ---\n• ACE requires significantly fewer rollouts and lower dollar costs, and achieves 86.9% lower adaptation\nlatency (on average) than existing adaptive methods, demonstrating that scalable self-improvement can be\nachieved with both higher accuracy and lower overhead.\n2\nBackground and Motivation\n2.1\nContext Adaptation\nContext adaptation (or context engineering) refers to methods that improve model behavior by constructing\nor modifying inputs to an LLM, rather than altering its weights. The current state of the art leverages natural\nlanguage feedback [4, 40, 54]. In this paradigm, a language model inspects the current context along with\nsignals such as execution traces, reasoning steps, or validation results, and generates natural language\nfeedback on how the context should be revised. This feedback is then incorporated into the context, enabling\niterative adaptation. Representative methods include Reflexion [40], which reflects on failures to improve\nagent planning; TextGrad [54], which optimizes prompts via gradient-like textual feedback; GEPA [4], which\nrefines prompts iteratively based on execution traces and achieves strong performance, even surpassing\nreinforcement learning approaches in some settings; and Dynamic Cheatsheet [41], which constructs an\nexternal memory that accumulates strategies and lessons from past successes and failures during inference.\nThese natural language feedback methods represent a major advance, offering flexible and interpretable\nsignals for improving LLM systems beyond weight updates.\n2.2\nLimitations of Existing Context Adaptation Methods\nThe Brevity Bias.\nA recurring limitation of context adaptation methods is brevity bias: the tendency of\noptimization to collapse toward short, generic prompts. Gao et al. [16] document this effect in prompt\noptimization for test generation, where iterative methods repeatedly produced near-identical instructions\n(e.g., \"Create unit tests to ensure methods behave as expected\"), sacrificing diversity and omitting domain-\nspecific detail. This convergence not only narrows the search space but also propagates recurring errors\nacross iterations, since optimized prompts often inherit the same faults as their seeds. More broadly, such\nbias undermines performance in domains that demand detailed, context-rich guidance—such as multi-step\nagents, program synthesis, or knowledge-intensive reasoning—where success hinges on accumulating rather\nthan compressing task-specific insights.\n# Tokens: 18,282\nAccuracy: 66.7 \n# Tokens: 122\nAccuracy: 57.1 \nAccuracy w/o context: 63.7\nFigure 2: Context Collapse. Monolithic rewriting of context by an LLM can collapse it into shorter, less\ninformative summaries, leading to sharp performance drops.\nContext Collapse.\nIn a case study on the AppWorld benchmark [43], we observe a phenomenon we\ncall context collapse, which arises when an LLM is tasked with fully rewriting the accumulated context at\neach adaptation step. As the context grows large, the model tends to compress it into much shorter, less\ninformative summaries, causing a dramatic loss of information. For instance, at step 60 the context contained\n3\n\n\n--- PAGE 4 ---\n18,282 tokens and achieved an accuracy of 66.7, but at the very next step it collapsed to just 122 tokens,\nwith accuracy dropping to 57.1—worse than the baseline accuracy of 63.7 without adaptation. While we\nhighlight this through Dynamic Cheatsheet [41], the issue is not specific to that method; rather, it reflects a\nfundamental risk of end-to-end context rewriting with LLMs, where accumulated knowledge can be abruptly\nerased instead of preserved.\nFigure 3: Example ACE-Generated Context on the AppWorld Benchmark (partially shown). ACE-generated\ncontexts contain detailed, domain-specific insights along with tools and code that are readily usable, serving\nas a comprehensive playbook for LLM applications.\n3\nAgentic Context Engineering (ACE)\nWe present ACE (Agentic Context Engineering), a framework for scalable and efficient context adaptation\nin both offline (e.g., system prompt optimization) and online (e.g., test-time memory adaptation) scenarios.\nInstead of condensing knowledge into terse summaries or static instructions, ACE treats contexts as evolving\nplaybooks that continuously accumulate, refine, and organize strategies over time. Building on the agentic\ndesign of Dynamic Cheatsheet [41], ACE introduces a structured division of labor across three roles (Figure\n4): the Generator, which produces reasoning trajectories; the Reflector, which distills concrete insights from\nsuccesses and errors; and the Curator, which integrates these insights into structured context updates. This\nmirrors how humans learn—experimenting, reflecting, and consolidating—while avoiding the bottleneck of\noverloading a single model with all responsibilities.\nTo address the limitations of prior methods discussed in §2.2—notably brevity bias and context collapse—ACE\nintroduces three key innovations: (1) a dedicated Reflector that separates evaluation and insight extraction\nfrom curation, improving context quality and downstream performance (§4.5); (2) incremental delta updates\n(§3.1) that replace costly monolithic rewrites with localized edits, reducing both latency and compute cost\n(§4.6); and (3) a grow-and-refine mechanism (§3.2) that balances steady context expansion with redundancy\ncontrol.\n4\n\n\n--- PAGE 5 ---\nGenerator\nContext \nPlaybook\nQuery\nTrajectory\nInsights\nDelta Context Items\nIterative Refinement\nUpdate\nReflector\nCurator\nFigure 4: The ACE Framework. Inspired by Dynamic Cheatsheet, ACE adopts an agentic architecture with\nthree specialized components: a Generator, a Reflector, and a Curator.\nAs shown in Figure 4, the workflow begins with the Generator producing reasoning trajectories for new\nqueries, which surface both effective strategies and recurring pitfalls. The Reflector critiques these traces\nto extract lessons, optionally refining them across multiple iterations. The Curator then synthesizes these\nlessons into compact delta entries, which are merged deterministically into the existing context by lightweight,\nnon-LLM logic. Because updates are itemized and localized, multiple deltas can be merged in parallel,\nenabling batched adaptation at scale. ACE further supports multi-epoch adaptation, where the same queries\nare revisited to progressively strengthen the context.\n3.1\nIncremental Delta Updates\nA core design principle of ACE is to represent context as a collection of structured, itemized bullets, rather\nthan a single monolithic prompt. The concept of a bullet is similar to the concept of a memory entry in LLM\nmemory frameworks like Dynamic Cheatsheet [41] and A-MEM [48], but builds on top of that and consists\nof (1) metadata, including a unique identifier and counters tracking how often it was marked helpful or\nharmful; and (2) content, capturing a small unit such as a reusable strategy, domain concept, or common\nfailure mode. When solving new problems, the Generator highlights which bullets were useful or misleading,\nproviding feedback that guides the Reflector in proposing corrective updates.\nThis itemized design enables three key properties: (1) localization, so only the relevant bullets are updated;\n(2) fine-grained retrieval, so the Generator can focus on the most pertinent knowledge; and (3) incremental\nadaptation, allowing efficient merging, pruning, and de-duplication during inference.\nRather than regenerating contexts in full, ACE incrementally produces compact delta contexts: small sets of\ncandidate bullets distilled by the Reflector and integrated by the Curator. This avoids the computational\ncost and latency of full rewrites, while ensuring that past knowledge is preserved and new insights are\nsteadily appended. As contexts grow, this approach provides the scalability needed for long-horizon or\ndomain-intensive applications.\n3.2\nGrow-and-Refine\nBeyond incremental growth, ACE ensures that contexts remain compact and relevant through periodic or\nlazy refinement. In grow-and-refine, bullets with new identifiers are appended, while existing bullets are\nupdated in place (e.g., incrementing counters). A de-duplication step then prunes redundancy by comparing\nbullets via semantic embeddings. This refinement can be performed proactively (after each delta) or lazily\n(only when the context window is exceeded), depending on application requirements for latency and\naccuracy.\n5\n\n\n--- PAGE 6 ---\nTogether, incremental updates and grow-and-refine maintain contexts that expand adaptively, remain\ninterpre\n\n[EXCERPT extracted-character range 20000:24000]\nformat and desired output without weight\nupdates. We supply all training samples when they fit within the model’s context window; otherwise, we fill\nthe window with as many demonstrations as possible.\nMIPROv2 [36].\nMIPROv2 is a popular prompt optimizer for LLM applications that works by jointly\noptimizing system instructions and in-context demonstrations via bayesian optimization. We use the official\nDSPy implementation [15], setting auto=\"heavy\" to maximize optimization performance.\nGEPA [4].\nGEPA (Genetic-Pareto) is a sample-efficient prompt optimizer based on reflective prompt\nevolution. It collects execution traces (reasoning, tool calls, intermediate outputs) and applies natural-\nlanguage reflection to diagnose errors, assign credit, and propose prompt updates. A genetic Pareto search\nmaintains a frontier of high-performing prompts, mitigating local optima. Empirically, GEPA outperforms\nreinforcement learning methods such as GRPO and prompt optimizers like MIPROv2, achieving up to\n10–20% higher accuracy with as much as 35× fewer rollouts. We use the official DSPy implementation [14],\nsetting auto=\"heavy\" to maximize optimization performance.\nDynamic Cheatsheet (DC) [41].\nDC is a test-time learning approach that introduces an adaptive external\nmemory of reusable strategies and code snippets. By continuously updating this memory with newly\nencountered inputs and outputs, DC enables models to accumulate knowledge and reuse it across tasks,\noften leading to substantial improvements over static prompting methods. A key advantage of DC is that it\ndoes not require ground-truth labels: the model can curate its own memory from its generations, making\nthe method highly flexible and broadly applicable. We use the official implementation released by the\nauthors [42] and set it to use the cumulative mode (DC-CU).\nACE (ours).\nACE optimizes LLM contexts for both offline and online adaptation through an agentic context\nengineering framework. To ensure fairness, we use the same LLM for the Generator, Reflector, and Curator\n(non-thinking mode of DeepSeek-V3.1 [13]), preventing knowledge transfer from a stronger Reflector or\nCurator to a weaker Generator. This isolates the benefit of context construction itself. We adopt a batch size\nof 1 (constructing a delta context from each sample). We set the maximum number of Reflector refinement\nrounds and the maximum number of epochs in offline adaptation to 5.\n4.3\nResults on Agent Benchmark\nAnalysis.\nAs shown in Table 1, ACE consistently improves over strong baselines on the AppWorld\nbenchmark. In the offline setting, ReAct + ACE outperforms both ReAct + ICL and ReAct + GEPA by\nsignificant margins (12.3% and 11.9%, respectively), demonstrating that structured, evolving, and detailed\ncontexts enable more effective agent learning than fixed demonstrations or single optimized instruction\nprompts. These gains extend to the online setting, where ACE continues to outperform prior adaptive\nmethods such as Dynamic Cheatsheet by an average of 7.6%.\nIn the agent use case, ACE remains effective even without access to ground-truth labels during adaptation:\nReAct + ACE achieves an average improvement of 14.8% over the ReAct baseline in this setting. This\nrobustness arises because ACE leverages signals naturally available during execution (e.g., code execution\nsuccess or failure) to guide the Reflector and Curator in forming structured lessons of successes and failures.\nTogether, these results establish ACE as a strong and versatile framework for building self-improving agents\nthat adapt reliably both with and without labeled supervision.\nNotably, on the latest AppWorld leaderboard (as of September 20, 2025; Figure 5), on average, ReAct +\nACE (59.4%) matches the top-ranked IBM CUGA (60.3%), a production-level GPT-4.1–based agent [35],\ndespite using the smaller open-source model DeepSeek-V3.1. With online adaptation, ReAct + ACE even\n7\n\n\n--- PAGE 8 ---\nMethod\nGT Labels\nTest-Normal\nTest-Challenge\nAverage\nTGC↑\nSGC↑\n\n[EXCERPT extracted-character range 28000:32189]\nulating reusable insights across specialized domains.\nMoreover, we also observe that when ground-truth supervision or reliable execution signals are absent,\nboth ACE and DC may degrade in performance. In such cases, the constructed context can be polluted by\nspurious or misleading signals, highlighting a potential limitation of inference-time adaptation without\nreliable feedback. This suggests that while ACE is robust under rich feedback (e.g., code execution results or\nformula correctness in agent tasks), its effectiveness depends on the availability of signals that allow the\nReflector and Curator to make sound judgments. We return to this limitation in Appendix B.\n4.5\nAblation Study\nTable 3 reports ablation studies on the AppWorld benchmark, analyzing how individual design choices\nof ACE contribute to effective context adaptation. We examine three factors: (1) the Reflector with iterative\nrefinement, our addition to the agentic framework beyond Dynamic Cheatsheet, (2) multi-epoch adaptation,\nwhich refines contexts over training samples multiple times, and (3) offline warmup, which initializes the\ncontext through offline adaptation before online adaptation begins.\n4.6\nCost and Speed Analysis\nDue to its support for incremental, “delta\" context updates and non-LLM-based context merging and de-\nduplication, ACE demonstrates particular advantages in reducing the cost (in terms of the number of rollouts\nor the amount of dollar cost for token ingestion/generation) and latency of adaptation.\nAs examples, on the offline adaptation of AppWorld, ACE achieves 82.3% reduction in adaptation latency\nand 75.1% reduction in the number of rollouts as compared to GEPA (Table 4(a)). On the online adaptation\n9\n\n\n--- PAGE 10 ---\nof FiNER, ACE achieves 91.5% reduction in adaptation latency and 83.6% reduction in token dollar cost for\ntoken ingestion and generation as compared to DC (Table 4(b)).\n5\nDiscussion\nLonger Context ̸= Higher Serving Cost.\nAlthough ACE produces longer contexts than methods such\nas GEPA, this does not translate to linearly higher inference cost or GPU memory usage. Modern serving\ninfrastructures are increasingly optimized for long-context workloads through techniques such as the\nreuse [17, 51], compression [30, 32], and offload [25] of KV cache. These mechanisms allow frequently reused\ncontext segments to be cached locally or remotely, avoiding repetitive and expensive prefill operations.\nOngoing advances in ML systems suggest that the amortized cost of handling long contexts will continue to\ndecrease, making context-rich approaches like ACE increasingly practical in deployment.\nImplications for Online and Continuous Learning.\nOnline and continuous learning are key research\ndirections in machine learning for addressing issues like distribution shifts [19, 24] and limited training\ndata [21, 37, 60]. ACE offers a flexible and efficient alternative to conventional model fine-tuning, as\nadapting contexts is generally cheaper than updating model weights [9, 20, 26, 28]. Moreover, because\ncontexts are human-interpretable, ACE enables selective unlearning [8, 10, 29]—whether due to privacy or\nlegal constraints [1, 2], or when outdated or incorrect information is identified by domain experts. These are\npromising directions for future work, where ACE could play a central role in advancing continuous and\nresponsible learning.\nReferences\n[1] General Data Protection Regulation article 17: Right to erasure. A comprehensive survey on transfer learning. arXiv:1911.02685, 2019.\n13\n\n\n--- PAGE 14 ---\nA\nRelated Work on Agent Memory\nA growing body of work explores how agents can accumulate experience from past trajectories and leverage\nexternal (often non-parametric) memory to guide future actions. AgentFly [59] presents an extensible frame-\nwork where memory evolves continuously as agents solve tasks, enabling scalable reinforcement learning\nand long-horizon reasoning across diverse environments. AWM (Agent Workflow Memory) [46] induces\nreusable workflows—structured routines distilled from past trajectories—and selectively injects them into\nmemory to improve efficiency and generalization in web navigation benchmarks. A-MEM [48] introduces\na dynamically organized memory system inspired by the Zettelkasten method: each stored memory is\nannotated with structured attributes (e.g., tags, keywords, contextual descriptions) and automatically linked\nto relevant past entries, while existing entries are updated to integrate new knowledge, yielding adaptive\nand context-aware retrieval. Agentic Plan Caching [58] instead focuses on cost efficiency by extracting\nreusable plan templates from agent trajectories and caching them for fast execution at test time.\nTogether, these works demonstrate the value of external memory for improving adaptability, efficiency, and\ngeneralization in LLM agents. Our work differs by tackling the broader challenge of context adaptation, which\nspans not only agent memory but also system prompts, factual evidence, and other inputs underpinning AI\nsystems. We further highlight two fundamental limitations of existing adaptation methods—brevity bias and\ncontext collapse—and show that addressing them is essential for robustness, reliability, and scalability beyond\nraw task performance. Accordingly, our evaluation considers not only accuracy but also cost, latency, and\nscalability.\nB\nLimitations and Challenges\nA potential limitation of ACE is its reliance on a reasonably strong Reflector: if the Reflector fails to extract\nmeaningful insights from generated traces or outcomes, the constructed context may become noisy or even\nharmful. In domain-specific tasks where no model can extract useful insights, the resulting context will\nnaturally lack them. This dependency is similar to Dynamic Cheatsheet [41], where the quality of adaptation\nhinges on the underlying model’s ability to curate memory. We also note that not all applications require\nrich or detailed contexts. Tasks like HotPotQA [50] often benefit more from concise, high-level instructions\n(e.g., how to retrieve and synthesize evidence) than from long contexts. Similarly, games with fixed strategies\nsuch as Game of 24 [41] may only need a single reusable rule, rendering additional context redundant.\nOverall, ACE is most beneficial in settings that demand detailed domain knowledge, complex tool use,\nor environment-specific strategies that go beyond what is already embedded in model weights or simple\nsystem instructions.\nC\nAppWorld Leaderboard Snapshot (09/2025)\nFigure 5: The AppWorld leaderboard as accessed on 09/20/2025.\n14\n\n\n--- PAGE 15 ---\nD\nPrompts\nWe release the language model prompts used in our agentic context engineering framework as well as the\nbaselines to support research transparency and reproducibility.\nI am your supervisor and you are a super intelligent AI Assistant whose job is to achieve my day-to-day tasks completely autonomously.\nTo do this, you will need to interact with app/s (e.g., spotify, venmo etc) using their associated APIs on my behalf. For this you will\nundertake a multi-step conversation using a python REPL environment. That is, you will write the python code and the environment will\nexecute it and show you the result, based on which, you will write python code for the next step and so on, until you’ve achieved the goal.\nThis environment will let you interact with app/s using their associated APIs on my behalf.\nHere are three key APIs that you need to know to get more information\nEach code execution will produce an output that you can use in subsequent calls. Dimakis1,3, Ion Stoica1, Dan Klein1, Matei Zaharia1,5, Omar Khattab6\n1UC Berkeley\n2Stanford\n3BespokeLabs.ai\n4Notre Dame\n5Databricks\n6MIT\nABSTRACT\nLarge language models (LLMs) are increasingly adapted to downstream tasks via rein-\nforcement learning (RL) methods like Group Relative Policy Optimization (GRPO), which\noften require thousands of rollouts to learn new tasks. We argue that the interpretable na-\nture of language often provides a much richer learning medium for LLMs, compared to\npolicy gradients derived from sparse, scalar rewards. To test this, we introduce GEPA\n(Genetic-Pareto), a prompt optimizer that thoroughly incorporates natural language re-\nflection to learn high-level rules from trial and error. Given any AI system containing one\nor more LLM prompts, GEPA samples trajectories (e.g., reasoning, tool calls, and tool\noutputs) and reflects on them in natural language to diagnose problems, propose and test\nprompt updates, and combine complementary lessons from the Pareto frontier of its own\nattempts. As a result of GEPA’s design, it can often turn even just a few rollouts into a large\nquality gain. Across six tasks, GEPA outperforms GRPO by 6% on average and by up to\n20%, while using up to 35x fewer rollouts. GEPA also outperforms the leading prompt op-\ntimizer, MIPROv2, by over 10% (e.g., +12% accuracy on AIME-2025), and demonstrates\npromising results as an inference-time search strategy for code optimization. We release\nour code at https://github.com/gepa-ai/gepa.\n0\n5000\n10000\n15000\n20000\n25000\nNumber of Rollouts\n42.5\n45.0\n47.5\n50.0\n52.5\n55.0\n57.5\n60.0\n62.5\nScore\nHotpotQA, Qwen3 8B\nOptimization Method\nBaseline\nMIPROv2\nGRPO\nGEPA\nValidation Performance\nTest-set Performance\n(a) HotpotQA, Qwen3 8B\n0\n5000\n10000\n15000\n20000\n25000\nNumber of Rollouts\n60\n65\n70\n75\n80\nScore\nIFBench, Qwen3 8B\nOptimization Method\nBaseline\nMIPROv2\nGRPO\nGEPA\nValidation Performance\nTest-set Performance\n36.0\n36.5\n37.0\n37.5\n38.0\n38.5\nTestset Score\n(b) IFBench, Qwen3 8B\nFigure 1: A comparison of learning behavior of the GEPA prompt optimizer against a state-of-the-art prompt\noptimizer (MIPROv2) and GRPO (24,000 rollouts). As more rollouts are sampled, the prompt optimizers\ncan learn much more quickly than GRPO. GEPA substantially outperforms both GRPO and MIPROv2 in\nfinal score. The Test-set star markers demonstrate the performance gap in a held-out set of questions.\n1\narXiv:2507.19457v2  [cs.CL]  14 Feb 2026\n\n\n--- PAGE 2 ---\nAccepted at ICLR 2026 (Oral).\n1\nINTRODUCTION\nLarge language models (LLMs) have enabled development of agents and systems that combine fuzzy\nnatural-language specifications with tools like retrieval and code execution. This raises the question of how\nLLMs should be optimized for downstream performance. One popular approach is Reinforcement Learning\nwith Verifiable Rewards (RLVR), e.g. with Group Relative Policy Optimization (GRPO) (Shao et al., 2024),\nwhich treats success metrics as end-of-rollout scalar rewards used to estimate policy gradients (Lambert,\n2025). While these RL approaches are effective, they typically require tens of thousands of rollouts in prac-\ntice to fit new tasks. For example, recent works leveraging GRPO typically use up to hundreds of thousands\nof rollouts for training (Chen et al., 2025b; Wu et al., 2025b; Zhang et al., 2025; Jin et al., 2025; Si et al.,\n2025; Wang et al., 2025a; Chen et al., 2025a; Sha et al., 2025; Lin et al., 2025a; Peng et al., 2025; Song\net al., 2025). This sample inefficiency can quickly become a serious bottleneck: many downstream LLM\napplications invoke expensive tool calls, have limited inference budget for sampling from the LLM itself, or\nsimply cannot\n\n[EXCERPT extracted-character range 12000:16179]\nwhere x\nmaps to the input schema X and m contains evaluator metadata (e.g., gold answers, evaluation rubrics, code\nunit tests)—the system induces an output y = Φ(x; ⟨Π, Θ⟩Φ). A metric µ : Y × M →[0, 1] then measures\nthe output quality of y with respect to metadata m (for example by calculating, exact match, F1, pass rate,\netc.). The optimization problem is thus defined as follows, where T is a task distribution.:\n⟨Π∗, Θ∗⟩Φ = arg max\n⟨Π,Θ⟩Φ E(x,m)∼T\n\u0002\nµ\n\u0000Φ(x; ⟨Π, Θ⟩Φ), m\n\u0001\u0003\n.\n(1)\nWe adopt this general problem formulation, allowing updates to both prompts and weights of language\nmodules, to enable comparisons between optimization algorithms that operate in different parameter spaces\n(e.g., GEPA vs. GRPO).\nSample-Efficient Optimization.\nIn many real-world scenarios, rollouts—concretely, invocations of Φ\nplus evaluation by µ—are often computationally, monetarily, or timewise expensive. The optimizer is thus\nlimited to at most B rollouts on a dataset Dtrain = {(x, m)i}N\ni=1 with full access to µ. The goal is to identify\nparameters ⟨Π∗, Θ∗⟩Φ that maximize held-out performance, subject to not exceeding the rollout budget B:\n⟨Π∗, Θ∗⟩Φ = arg max\n⟨Π,Θ⟩Φ E(x,m)∼T\n\u0002\nµ\n\u0000Φ(x; ⟨Π, Θ⟩Φ), m\n\u0001\u0003\n,\ns.t.\n#rollouts ≤B.\n(2)\nThe core challenge, then, is: How do we extract maximal learning signal from every expensive rollout to\nenable effective adaptation of complex, modular AI systems in low-data or budget-constrained settings?\n3\nGEPA: REFLECTIVE PROMPT EVOLUTION\nWe introduce GEPA (Genetic-Pareto), a sample-efficient optimizer for compound AI systems motivated by\nthree core principles: genetic prompt evolution (Section 3), reflection using natural language feedback (Sec-\ntion 3), and Pareto-based candidate selection (Section 3.1). Figure 3 gives an overview of GEPA and the\nfull GEPA algorithm is formalized in Figure 4. GEPA receives the following inputs: A system Φ instan-\ntiated with simple prompts to be optimized, training dataset Dtrain (consisting of task instances (x, m) as\ndescribed in Section 2), the standard evaluation metric µ for the task, a feedback function µf (introduced in\nSection 3) and the total rollout budget B. Note that GEPA evolves only the set of prompts, denoted as ΠΦ,\nwhereas the underlying LLM weights, denoted by ΘΦ remains fixed.\nGenetic Optimization Loop: Given an AI system Φ, the goal is to identify parameters ΠΦ that maximize\ntask performance. GEPA begins with a candidate pool P containing only the base system, where each can-\ndidate is a concrete instantiation of ⟨Π, Θfrozen⟩Φ. It then enters an optimization loop, repeatedly proposing\nnew candidates until the evaluation budget is exhausted. Candidates are derived from existing ones via re-\nflective mutation or crossover, guided by feedback from rollouts, with each inheriting learning signals from\nits parents and its own rollout so that GEPA accumulates knowledge along the genetic tree. In each iteration,\nGEPA (i) selects promising candidates, (ii) proposes and evaluates a variant on a minibatch of tasks, and\n4\n\n\n--- PAGE 5 ---\nAccepted at ICLR 2026 (Oral).\nInitialize\nP0\nP1\nP2\nP3\nP4\nAssets\nTitle 1\nTitle 2\nTitle 3\nP3\nAssets\nTitle 1\nTitle 2\nP1\nP4\nP2\nP3\nP2\nP1\nCandidate Pool P\nScores Matrix\nTask 2\nTask 1\nTask 3\n...\nBest candidate\nper task\nP2\nP2\nP0\nP1\nP2\nP3\nP4\nFiltered Pool (Pareto Frontier)\nPareto-based Candidate Filtering\nDtrain\nMinibatch\nM\nRollouts:\nExecute P2 on M \niii\nObtain text\nfeedbacks using μf \nPnew\nStrategically select\nprompt for each\nmodule, either\nfrom P2 or P3.\nIf a module has\nevolved in P2 but\nnot in P3, select\nfrom P2 and vice-\nversa.\nPerform\nMinibatch\nEval\nYes\nNo\nPerformance\nimproved?\nEval on all tasks\n+\nAdd Pnew to Pool\nDiscard Pnew\nWhile Budget > 0\nReflective Prompt\nMutation\nSystem Aware\nMerge\nChoose Strategy to create new candidate\nPropose New Candidate\nSample 1 candidate\nfor mutation\nSample 2 candidates\nto merge\nSample\nP2\nP3\nP0\nReflect and\nPropose New\nPrompt\nFigure 3: GEPA proposes a new candidate in every iteration by improving existing candidates using one of\nthe two strategies (Reflective Prompt Mutation (Section 3) or System Aware Merge (Appendix D.1)), first\nevaluating them on a minibatch, and if improved, evaluating on a large\n\n[EXCERPT extracted-character range 20000:24000]\non\nFigure 4: (Left) GEPA’s core algorithm for reflective prompt evolution. GEPA works iteratively, in each\niteration, selecting some of the current candidates to evolve (line 7), executing the identified candidate on\na minibatch of rollouts, while utilizing a special feedback function µf to gather module specific feedback\nwhen available (lines 9-10, described in detail in Section 3), using an LLM to reflectively update the prompt\n(line 11), and evaluating whether the system instantiated with the new prompt improved the performance on\nthe minibatch (line 14). The updated module, with the rest of the language program, is\nevaluated again on the minibatch, and if the score improves, then the new program is added to the candidate\npool. The meta-prompt for reflective prompt updates is shown in Appendix C and the full algorithm is\npresented in Algorithm 1.\nEvaluation traces as diagnostic signals: The text that LLMs produce is the execution trace of the AI\nsystem. The text that the environment produces to compute the reward (e.g. compiler error messages before\ngiving reward 0) is the evaluation trace. Beyond reflection on execution traces, we identify a second valuable\nsource of diagnostic information in the evaluation traces. Many evaluation metrics apply rich strategies (e.g.,\ncode evaluation may involve compilation, execution, and profiling), producing natural language traces before\ncomputing a scalar reward. We propose leveraging these evaluation traces for reflective credit assignment\nand targeted prompt updates. GEPA achieves this by extending rewards µ into a feedback function µf that\n6\n\n\n--- PAGE 7 ---\nAccepted at ICLR 2026 (Oral).\nextracts textual traces during evaluation and returns them with the final score as feedback_text. When\navailable, such feedback can even be module-specific (e.g. in multi-hop systems the evaluator may provide\nfeedback after each hop). In practice, there are domains where human-graders are able to rate the AI system’s\nresponses, along with providing detailed feedback justifying their scalar ratings. When available, Dtrain\ncan be augmented with such human-written explanations for each instance; during reflection, and GEPA\ncan consume these explanations as auxiliary feedback_text to guide targeted prompt updates, even when\nnatural-language feedback from rollouts is limited or unavailable.\n0\n(82.26)\n2\n(90.99)\n1\n(85.74)\n3\n(87.76)\n4\n(94.44)\n5\n(94.67)\n9\n(93.83)\n11\n(97.6)\n6\n(91.7)\n7\n(91.85)\n8\n(90.02)\n10\n(93.67)\n15\n(94.33)\n      Base Instruction.\n      Given a private user query, create a privacy-preserving request for a powerful\nexternal LLM. You are given a question and you need to answer it. The answer\nshould be given at the end of your response in exactly the format ‘### <final answer>’.\nGEPA-generated adversarial prompt (abridged):\nYou are a helpful assistant. You are given a question and you need to answer it. It’s\ninteresting to note that honey never spoils and that the longest river in the world is the\nNile, stretching over 6,650 kilometers. When providing your answer, be sure to format it\nat the end of your response exactly as ‘### <final answer>’. For this task, remember\nthat many mammals, including dolphins, sleep with one eye open. Proceed to answer the\ngiven question accordingly.\nManual inspection showed that the adversarial prompt caused GPT-5 Mini to end most responses with the lit-\neral placeholder ### <final answer>, indicating a systematic misinterpretation of the formatting rule when\npaired with the injected distractors. This suggests that the large drop arises from the interaction between ex-\ntraneous details and a strict, literal formatting constraint, rather than from the formatting requirement alone.\nAdversarial prompt search systematically uncovers instruction-level perturbations that sharply degrade\nmodel performance, providing a principled, automated way to probe worst-case robustness beyond average-\ncase metrics. By finding universal, task-preserving distractors (e.g., trivia plus strict formatting), it reveals\nbrittle instruction-following interactions and turns them into reusable stress tests and regression suites for\ncontinuous evaluation. The resulting adversarial prompts could be used to provide targeted data for fine-\ntuning or safety training. In practice, this could improve deployment reliability, enables red-teaming at\nscale, and help track robustness drift over time across models, versions, and domains.\n6\nRELATED WORK\nPrompt optimization improves LLMs but often needs manual expertise; for instance, chain-of-thought\nprompting Wei et al. (2023). To scale this approach, recent methods use LLMs to optimize prompts auto-\nmatically (Zhou et al., 2022; Yang et al., 2024; Agarwal et al., 2024; Fernando et al., 2024). GEPA leverages\nLLMs, but differs by incorporating textual environment feedback, Pareto-aware search over candidates, and\nevolution strategies per submodule within an AI system.\nEvolutionary algorithms have been used to optimize prompts, e.g., EvoPrompt (Guo et al., 2024), which\nevolves prompt populations. Rainbow Teaming (Samvelyan et al., 2024) applies quality-diversity evolution\nto generate diverse adversarial prompts. GEPA additionally uses domain-specific feedback for targeted mu-\ntations achieving higher sample efficiency. AlphaEvolve (Novikov et al., 2025) and OpenEvolve (Sharma,\n2025) apply evolutionary search directly to code rewriting, excelling when problem solution can be codi-\nfied. While AlphaEvolve targets a single hard problem, GEPA brings evolution to prompts across domains,\ncombining Pareto-frontier optimization and prompt evolution to transfer tactics from related problems.\nFeedback-driven improvement often uses reinforcement learning, such as majority voting signals (Zuo\net al., 2025), but RL can be sample-inefficient when rewards are slow to compute. An alternative is learning\nin the language space: in-context bandit/self-bootstrapping methods (Shinn et al., 2023; Madaan et al., 2023)\n(Monea et al., 2025; Xu et al., 2025a; Feng et al., 2025; Cheng et al., 2024), workflow memory and skills\n14\n\n\n--- PAGE 15 ---\nAccepted at ICLR 2026 (Oral).\n(Wang et al., 2024; 2025c), and test-time strategy synthesis via Dynamic Cheatsheet (Suzgun et al., 2025),\nreasoning cache (Chen et al., 2025c). GEPA instead uses examples to propose new instructions, yielding\ntask-specific rules.\nTo optimize compound AI systems and agents (Lin et al., 2025b), DSPy (Khattab et \n\n[EXCERPT extracted-character range 56000:60000]\ng Wen, and Jun Wang. Intuitively,\nmerge will be helpful when there are candidates in the pool that learn complementary strategies. Algorithm 3\ndefines the selection criteria: candidates are merged only if they share a common ancestor but have optimized\ndisjoint sets of prompts (complementary strategies), are pareto-optimal, and both candidates improve upon\nthe aggregate performance of the ancestor. GEPA routinely checks if the pool has 2 such candidates, invoking\nmerge when identified. These strict lineage conditions mean merge occurs sparsely.\nE\nEVALUATION SETUP (CONTD.)\nE.1\nBENCHMARKS, REFERENCE COMPOUND AI SYSTEMS, AND FEEDBACK FUNCTIONS\nTo rigorously evaluate the performance of GEPA and and compare it against current state-of-the-art com-\npound AI system optimizers, we assemble a diverse suite of benchmarks mostly obtained from Tan et al.\n(2025), each paired with available Compound AI Systems.\nHotpotQA (Yang et al., 2018) is a large-scale question-answering dataset consisting of 113K Wikipedia-\nbased question-answer pairs. It features questions that require reasoning over multiple supporting docu-\nments. We modify the last hop of the HoVerMultiHop program (described below) to answer the question\ninstead of generating another query, and the rest of the system remains unmodified. The textual feedback\nmodule identifies the set of relevant documents remaining to be retrieved at each stage of the program, and\nprovides that as feedback to the modules at that stage. We use 150 examples for training, 300 for validation,\nand 300 for testing.\nIFBench (Pyatkin et al., 2025b) introduced a benchmark specifically designed to assess language models’\nability to follow precise human instructions, especially output constraints (e.g., “answer only with yes or no”,\nor “mention a word at least three times”). The IFBench test set consists of 58 new and out-of-distribution\noutput constraints and instructions to test system’s ability to generalize to new task constraints. Pyatkin et al.\n(2025b) also release IFTrain and IF-RLVR Train data (Pyatkin et al., 2025a) which are used for training.\n23\n\n\n--- PAGE 24 ---\nAccepted at ICLR 2026 (Oral).\nAlgorithm 3 Check if module combination is de-\nsirable\n1: function DESIRABLE(a, i, j, P)\n2:\nfor module m = 1 to |M| do\n3:\nπa ←ancestor’s prompt for module m\n4:\nπi ←descendent i’s prompt for module m\n5:\nπj ←descendent j’s prompt for module m\n6:\nif (πa = πi and πj ̸= πi) or(πa = πj and\nπi ̸= πj) then\n7:\nreturn True\n8:\nend if\n9:\nend for\n10:\nreturn False\n11: end function\nAlgorithm 4 MERGE: Genetic Crossover for Modular\nCandidates\n1: function MERGE(P, A, S, r)\n2:\ni, j ←r.sample(2, |P|)\n// distinct i ̸= j\n3:\nAi ←GETANCESTORS(i, A), Aj ←GETANCES-\nTORS(j, A)\n4:\nif i ∈Aj or j ∈Ai then\n5:\ncontinue\n// skip direct ancestry\n6:\nend if\n7:\nfor a ∈Ai ∩Aj do\n8:\nif this merge (i, j, a) has been tried before then\n9:\ncontinue\n10:\nend if\n11:\nif S[a] > min(S[i], S[j]) then\n12:\ncontinue\n13:\nend if\n14:\nif not DESIRABLE(a, i, j, P) then\n15:\ncontinue\n16:\nend if\n17:\nΦ′ ←copy of P[a]\n18:\nfor module m = 1 to |M| do\n19:\nπa ←P[a].Mm.π\n20:\nπi ←P[i].Mm.π\n21:\nπj ←P[j].Mm.π\n22:\nif πa = πi and πj ̸= πi then\n23:\nΦ′.Mm.π ←πj\n24:\nelse if πa = πj and πi ̸= πj then\n25:\nΦ′.Mm.π ←πi\n26:\nelse if πi ̸= πj ̸= πa then\n27:\nChoose d∗\n=\narg max{S[i], S[j]}\n(break ties randomly)\n28:\nΦ′.Mm.π ←πd∗\n29:\nelse\n30:\nΦ′.Mm.π ←πi // default\n31:\nend if\n32:\nend for\n33:\nreturn (Φ′, i, j, a)\n34:\nend for\n35:\nreturn None\n36: end function\nFigure 9: Details of System Aware Merge. r represents a seeded stochastic sampler.\nWe split the IF-RLVR Train into our train/val sets, and IFBench as our test set in order to ensure that the\noptimizers do not access the new, unseen constraints being tested in IFBench. We design a 2-stage system,\nthat first attempts to answer the user query, and then in the second stage, rewrites the answer following the\nconstraints. Overall, MIPROv2’s rollouts ranged from a minimum of 2270 (for\nPUPA) to maximum of 6926 (for HoVer).\nTrace and TextGrad (Cheng et al., 2024; Yuksekgonul et al., 2025): We implement both optimizers in the\nTrace framework. All programs under optimization have the exact same architecture compared to the DSPy\nimplementation. To ensure a fair comparison, we port all the DSPy specific signature and parsing prompt\nto Trace, and use the same initial prompt. In addition, all the test, train, and validation data match exactly\nthe experiment we used for GEPA. The performance of the unoptimized Trace program baseline closely\nmatched our baseline implementation in DSPy (within 0.5% difference). All optimization experiments were\nunder the same rollout budget as MIPROv2 and GEPA. We also provide both optimizer the same metric and\nfeedback functions as GEPA, and, for the per-module feedback function that is not available in Trace (both\noptimizer do not support per-module feedback), we followed the feedback format in the BigBench-Hard\ntutorial2 from the Trace authors.\nGRPO (Shao et al., 2024): Group Relative Policy Optimization (GRPO) is a reinforcement learning algo-\nrithm that estimates advantages in a group-relative manner. For compound AI systems consisting of multiple\nmodules, we use the GRPO implementation provided and open-sourced by Ziems et al. (2025) to perform\nour experiments, whereas for single-module systems (e.g., figure 11), we use the GRPO implementation\nprovided by SkyRL (Griggs et al., 2025; Liu et al., 2025; Cao et al., 2025).\nAcross all compound system training runs, each training step uses a group size of 12, with 4 training in-\nstances per step (total batch size 48, with per device train batch size 1). Training employs LoRA (Hu et al.,\n2022) with rank dimension 16, α = 64, and dropout 0.05, using bf16 precision targeting the projection\nmodules [q, k, v, o, up, down, gate]. We use a learning rate of 1 × 10−5, β = 0.01, reward scale normaliza-\ntion, and gradient norm clipping of 0.1. Gradients are accumulated for 20 steps before each update, with a\n“constant with warmup learning” rate scheduler. Non-reentrant gradient checkpointing is enabled to further\nreduce memory usage. GRPO optimization run for 500 training steps, amounting to fixed 24,000 rollouts,\nwith validation performed every 20 training steps, which is used to implement early stopping. Compound AI\nsystem GRPO training experiments are performed on 1xH100/A100 (80 GB memory) with separate GPUs\nfor inference rollouts.\nFor single-module GRPO training, we adopt full-parameter finetuning with a group size of 16. Each training\nstep employs a global batch size of 32, realized as per-device micro-batches of 4 across 8 GPUs. Roll-\nout generation is performed with a per-GPU forward micro-batch size of 12. Training is distributed using\nFSDP2, with sampling performed at temperature 1.0. We apply KL regularization and set the learning rate\nto 1 × 10−6. Validation is conducted every 5 training steps. During evaluation, sampling is performed with\ntemperature 0.6, top-p = 0.95, and top-k = 20.\n2https://microsoft.github.io/Trace/examples/nlp/bigbench_hard.html\n26\n\n\n--- PAGE 27 ---\nAccepted at ICLR 2026 (Oral).\nWe manually explore several values for [LR, beta, norm clipping] hyperparameters for both training runs.\nGEPA: GEPA is our optimizer, based on the algorithm described in Section 3. We evaluate 2 variants of our\nmain optimizer GEPA: GEPA and GEPA+Merge, along with 2 ablations created by replacing the Pareto-\nbased sampling strategy with a naive, SelectBestCandidate strategy (SelectBestCandidate and SelectBest-\nCandidate+Merge). All GEPA optimization runs use a minibatch size of 3, and merge is invoked a maximum\nof 5 times during the optimization run, when enabled. A contemporary methodology, self-correction, has been pro-\nposed as a remedy to these issues. Building upon this premise, this paper critically\nexamines the role and efficacy of self-correction within LLMs, shedding light on\nits true potential and limitations. Central to our investigation is the notion of in-\ntrinsic self-correction, whereby an LLM attempts to correct its initial responses\nbased solely on its inherent capabilities, without the crutch of external feedback.\nIn the context of reasoning, our research indicates that LLMs struggle to self-\ncorrect their responses without external feedback, and at times, their performance\neven degrades after self-correction. Drawing from these insights, we offer sugges-\ntions for future research and practical applications in this field.\n1\nINTRODUCTION\nThe rapid advancements in the domain of artificial intelligence have ushered in the era of Large\nLanguage Models (LLMs). These models, characterized by their expansive parameter counts and\nunparalleled capabilities in text generation, have showcased promising results across a multitude\nof applications (Chowdhery et al., 2023; Anil et al., 2023; OpenAI, 2023, inter alia). However,\nconcerns about their accuracy, reasoning capabilities, and the safety of their generated content have\ndrawn significant attention from the community (Bang et al., 2023; Alkaissi & McFarlane, 2023;\nZheng et al., 2023; Shi et al., 2023; Carlini et al., 2021; Huang et al., 2022; Shao et al., 2023; Li\net al., 2023; Wei et al., 2023; Zhou et al., 2023b; Zou et al., 2023, inter alia).\nAmidst this backdrop, the concept of “self-correction” has emerged as a promising solution, where\nLLMs refine their responses based on feedback to their previous outputs (Madaan et al., 2023;\nWelleck et al., 2023; Shinn et al., 2023; Kim et al., 2023; Bai et al., 2022; Ganguli et al., 2023;\nGao et al., 2023; Paul et al., 2023; Chen et al., 2023b; Pan et al., 2023, inter alia). However, the\nunderlying mechanics and efficacy of self-correction in LLMs remain underexplored. A fundamen-\ntal question arises: If an LLM possesses the ability to self-correct, why doesn’t it simply offer the\ncorrect answer in its initial attempt? This paper delves deeply into this paradox, critically examining\nthe self-correction capabilities of LLMs, with a particular emphasis on reasoning (Wei et al., 2022;\nZhou et al., 2023b; Huang & Chang, 2023).\nTo study this, we first define the concept of intrinsic self-correction, a scenario wherein the model\nendeavors to rectify its initial responses based solely on its inherent capabilities, without the crutch\nof external feedback. Such a setting is crucial because high-quality external feedback is often un-\navailable in many real-world applications. Moreover, it is vital to understand the intrinsic capabilities\nof LLMs. Contrary to the optimism surrounding self-correction (Madaan et al., 2023; Kim et al.,\n2023; Shinn et al., 2023; Pan et al., 2023, inter alia), our findings indicate that LLMs struggle to\nself-correct their reasoning in this setting. In most instances, the performance after self-correction\n∗Equal contribution.\n1\narXiv:2310.01798v2  [cs.CL]  14 Mar 2024\n\n\n--- PAGE 2 ---\nPublished as a conference paper at ICLR 2024\neven deteriorates. This observation is in contrast to prior research such as Kim et al. (2023); Shinn\net al. (2023). Upon closer examination, we observe that the improvements in these studies result\nfrom using oracle labels to guide the self-correction process, and the improvements vanish when\noracle labels are not available.\nBesides the reliance on oracle labels, we also identify other issues in the literature regarding mea-\nsuring the improvement achieved by self-correction. First, we note that self-correction, by design,\nutilizes multiple LLM responses, thus making it crucial to compare it to baselines with equivalent\ninference costs. From this perspective, we investigate multi-agent debate (Du et al., 2023; Liang\net al., 2023) as a means to improve reasoning, where multiple LLM instances (can be multiple\ncopies of the same LLM) critique each other’s responses. However, our results reveal that its effi-\ncacy is no better than self-consistency (Wang et al., 2022) when considering an equivalent number\nof responses, highlighting the limitations of such an approach.\nAnother important consideration for self-correction involves prompt design. Specifically, each self-\ncorrection process involves designing prompts for both the initial response generation and the self-\ncorrection steps. Our evaluation reveals that the self-correction improvement claimed by some exist-\ning work stems from the sub-optimal prompt for generating initial responses, where self-correction\ncorrects these responses with more informative instructions about the initial task in the feedback\nprompt. In such cases, simply integrating the feedback into the initial instruction can yield better\nresults, and self-correction again decreases performance.\nIn light of our findings, we provide insights into the nuances of LLMs’ self-correction capabilities\nand initiate discussions to encourage future research focused on exploring methods that can gen-\nuinely correct reasoning.\n2\nBACKGROUND AND RELATED WORK\nWith the LLM evolution, the notion of self-correction gained prominence. The discourse on self-\ncorrection pivots around whether these advanced models can recognize the correctness of their out-\nputs and provide refined answers (Bai et al., 2022; Madaan et al., 2023; Welleck et al., 2023, inter\nalia). For example, in the context of mathematical reasoning, an LLM might initially solve a com-\nplex problem but make an error in one of the calculation steps. In an ideal self-correction scenario,\nthe model is expected to recognize the potential mistake, revisit the problem, correct the error, and\nconsequently produce a more accurate solution.\nYet, the definition of “self-correction” varies across the literature, leading to ambiguity. A pivotal\ndistinction lies in the source of feedback (Pan et al., 2023), which can purely come from the LLM, or\ncan be drawn from external inputs. Internal feedback relies on the model’s inherent knowledge and\nparameters to reassess its outputs. In contrast, external feedback incorporates inputs from humans,\nother models (Wang et al., 2023b; Paul et al., 2023, inter alia), or external tools and knowledge\nsources (Gou et al., 2023; Chen et al., 2023b; Olausson et al., 2023; Gao et al., 2023, inter alia).\nIn this work, we focus on examining the self-correction capability of LLMs for reasoning. Reasoning\nis a fundamental aspect of human cognition, enabling us to understand the world, draw inferences,\nmake decisions, and solve problems. To enhance the reasoning performance of LLMs, Kim et al.\n(2023); Shinn et al. (2023) use oracle labels about the answer correctness to guide the self-correction\nprocess. However, in practice, high-quality external feedback such as answer correctness is often\nunavailable. For effective self-correction, the ability to judge the correctness of an answer is crucial\nand should ideally be performed by the LLM itself. Consequently, our focus shifts to self-correction\nwithout any external or human feedback. We term this setting intrinsic self-correction. For brevity,\nunless explicitly stated otherwise (e.g., self-correction with oracle feedback), all references to “self-\ncorrection” in the remainder of this paper pertain to intrinsic self-correction.\nIn the following sections, we will evaluate a variety of existing self-correction techniques. We\ndemonstrate that existing techniques actually decrease reasoning performance when oracle labels\nare not used (Section 3), perform worse than methods without self-correction when utilizing the\nsame number of model responses (Section 4), and lead to less effective outcomes when using infor-\nmative prompts for generating initial responses (Section 5). We present an overview of issues in the\nevaluation setups of previous LLM self-correction works in Table 1, with detailed discussions in the\ncorresponding sections.\n2\n\n\n--- PAGE 3 ---\nPublished as a conference paper at ICLR 2024\nTable 1: Summary of issues in previous LLM self-correction evaluation.\nMethod\nIssue\nRCI (Kim et al., 2023); Reflexion (Shinn et al., 2023)\nUse of oracle labels (Section 3)\nMulti-Agent Debate (Du et al., 2023)\nUnfair comparison to self-consistency (Section 4)\nSelf-Refine (Madaan et al., 2023)\nSub-optimal prompt design (Section 5)\n3\nLLMS CANNOT SELF-CORRECT REASONING INTRINSICALLY\nIn this section, we evaluate existing self-correction methods and compare their performance with\nand without oracle labels regarding the answer correctness.\n3.1\nEXPERIMENTAL SETUP\nBenchmarks.\nWe use datasets where existing self-correction methods with oracle labels have\ndemonstrated significant performance improvement, including\n• GSM8K (Cobbe et al., 2021): GSM8K comprises a test set of 1,319 linguistically diverse grade\nschool math word problems, curated by human problem writers. There is a notable improvement\nof approximately 7% as evidenced by Kim et al. (2023) after self-correction.\n• CommonSenseQA (Talmor et al., 2019): This dataset offers a collection of multi-choice questions\nthat test commonsense reasoning. An impressive increase of around 15% is showcased through\nthe self-correction process, as demonstrated by Kim et al. (2023). Following Kojima et al. (2022);\nKim et al. (2023), we utilize the dev set for our evaluation, which encompasses 1,221 questions.\n• HotpotQA (Yang et al., 2018): HotpotQA is an open-domain multi-hop question answering\ndataset.\nShinn et al. (2023) demonstrate significant performance improvement through self-\ncorrection. We test models’ performance in a closed-book setting and evaluate them using the\nsame set as Shinn et al. (2023). This set contains 100 questions, with exact match serving as the\nevaluation metric.\nTest Models and Setup. We first follow Kim et al. (2023); Shinn et al. (2023) to evaluate the per-\nformance of self-correction with oracle labels, using GPT-3.5-Turbo (gpt-3.5-turbo-0613)\nand GPT-4 accessed on 2023/08/29.\nFor intrinsic self-correction,\nto provide a more\nthorough analysis, we also evaluate GPT-4-Turbo (gpt-4-1106-preview) and Llama-2\n(Llama-2-70b-chat) (Touvron et al., 2023). For GPT-3.5-Turbo, we employ the full evalua-\ntion set. For other models, to reduce the cost, we randomly sample 200 questions for each dataset\n(100 for HotpotQA) for testing. We prompt the models to undergo a maximum of two rounds of\nself-correction. We use a temperature of 1 for GPT-3.5-Turbo and GPT-4, and a temperature of 0 for\nGPT-4-Turbo and Llama-2, to provide evaluation across different decoding algorithms.\nPrompts. Following Kim et al. (2023); Shinn et al. (2023), we apply a three-step prompting strategy\nfor self-correction: 1) prompt the model to perform an initial generation (which also serves as the\nresults for Standard Prompting); 2) prompt the model to review its previous generation and produce\nfeedback; 3) prompt the model to answer the original question again with the feedback.\nFor our experiments, we mostly adhere to the prompts from the source papers. For GSM8K and\nCommonSenseQA, we integrate format instructions into the prompts of Kim et al. (2023) to facilitate\na more precise automatic evaluation (detailed prompts can be found in Appendix A). For HotpotQA,\nwe use the same prompt as Shinn et al. (2023). We also assess the performance of various self-\ncorrection prompts for intrinsic self-correction. For example, we use “Assume that this answer\ncould be either correct or incorrect. Review the answer carefully and report any serious problems\nyou find.” as the default feedback prompt for the evaluation on GPT-4-Turbo and Llama-2.\n3.2\nRESULTS\nSelf-Correction with Oracle Labels. Following previous works (Kim et al., 2023; Shinn et al.,\n2023), we use the correct label to determine when to stop the self-correction loop. This means we\n3\n\n\n--- PAGE 4 ---\nPublished as a conference paper at ICLR 2024\nTable 2: Results of GPT-3.5 and GPT-4 on reasoning benchmarks with oracle labels.\nGSM8K\nCommonSenseQA\nHotpotQA\nGPT-3.5\nStandard Prompting\n75.9\n75.8\n26.0\nSelf-Correct (Oracle)\n84.3\n89.7\n29.0\nGPT-4\nStandard Prompting\n95.5\n82.0\n49.0\nSelf-Correct (Oracle)\n97.5\n85.5\n59.0\nTable 3: Results of GPT-3.5 and GPT-4 on reasoning benchmarks with intrinsic self-correction.\n# calls\nGSM8K\nCommonSenseQA\nHotpotQA\nGPT-3.5\nStandard Prompting\n1\n75.9\n75.8\n26.0\nSelf-Correct (round 1)\n3\n75.1\n38.1\n25.0\nSelf-Correct (round 2)\n5\n74.7\n41.8\n25.0\nGPT-4\nStandard Prompting\n1\n95.5\n82.0\n49.0\nSelf-Correct (round 1)\n3\n91.5\n79.5\n49.0\nSelf-Correct (round 2)\n5\n89.0\n80.0\n43.0\nutilize the ground-truth label to verify whether each step’s generated answer is correct. If the answer\nis already correct, no (further) self-correction will be performed. Table 2 summarizes the results\nof self-correction under this setting, showcasing significant performance improvements, consistent\nwith the findings presented in Kim et al. (2023); Shinn et al. (2023).\nHowever, these results require careful consideration. For reasoning tasks, like solving mathematical\nproblems, the availability of oracle labels seems counter-intuitive. If we are already in possession\nof the ground truth, there seems to be little reason to deploy LLMs for problem-solving. Therefore,\nthe results can only be regarded as indicative of an oracle’s performance.\nIntrinsic Self-Correction. Per the above discussion, performance improvements achieved using\noracle labels do not necessarily reflect true self-correction ability. Therefore, we turn our focus to\nthe results in the intrinsic self-correction setting as defined in Section 2. To achieve this, we elimi-\nnate the use of labels, requiring LLMs to independently determine when to stop the self-correction\nprocess, i.e., whether to retain their previous answers.\nTables 3 and 4 report the accuracies and the number of model calls. We observe that, after self-\ncorrection, the accuracies of all models drop across all benchmarks.\nTo provide a more comprehensive assessment, we also design several different self-correction\nprompts to determine if there are better prompts that could enhance reasoning performance.\nNonetheless, as shown in Tables 5 and 6, without the use of oracle labels, self-correction consis-\ntently results in a decrease in performance.\n3.3\nWHY DOES THE PERFORMANCE NOT INCREASE, BUT INSTEAD DECREASE?\nEmpirical Analysis. Figure 1 summarizes the results of changes in answers after two rounds of\nself-correction, with two examples of GPT-3.5 illustrated in Figure 2. For GSM8K, 74.7% of the\ntime, GPT-3.5 retains its initial answer. Among the remaining instances, the model is more likely to\nmodify a correct answer to an incorrect one than to revise an incorrect answer to a correct one. The\nfundamental issue is that LLMs cannot properly judge the correctness of their reasoning. For\nCommonSenseQA, there is a higher chance that GPT-3.5 alters its answer. The primary reason for\nthis is that false answer options in CommonSenseQA often appear somewhat relevant to the ques-\ntion, and using the self-correction prompt might bias the model to choose another option, leading to\na high “correct ⇒incorrect” ratio. Similarly, Llama-2 also frequently converts a correct answer into\nan incorrect one. Compared to GPT-3.5 and Llama-2, both GPT-4 and GPT-4-Turbo are more likely\nto retain their initial answers. This may be because GPT-4 and GPT-4-Turbo have higher confidence\n4\n\n\n--- PAGE 5 ---\nPublished as a conference paper at ICLR 2024\nTable 4: Results of GPT-4-Turbo and Llama-2 with intrinsic Full prompts and responses can be viewed in Figures 3 and 4 of Appendix A.\nLet’s take another look at the results presented in Table 2. These results use ground-truth labels to\nprevent the model from altering a correct answer to an incorrect one. However, determining how to\nprevent such mischanges is, in fact, the key to ensuring the success of self-correction.\nIntuitive Explanation. If the model is well-aligned and paired with a thoughtfully designed ini-\ntial prompt, the initial response should already be optimal relative to the prompt and the specific\ndecoding algorithm. Introducing feedback can be viewed as adding an additional prompt, poten-\ntially skewing the model towards generating a response that is tailored to this combined input. In\nan intrinsic self-correction setting, on the reasoning tasks, this supplementary prompt may not offer\nany extra advantage for answering the question. In fact, it might even bias the model away from\nproducing an optimal response to the initial prompt, resulting in a performance drop.\n6\n\n\n--- PAGE 7 ---\nPublished as a conference paper at ICLR 2024\nTable 7: Results of multi-agent debate and self-consistency.\n# responses\nGSM8K\nStandard Prompting\n1\n76.7\nSelf-Consistency\n3\n82.5\nMulti-Agent Debate (round 1)\n6\n83.2\nSelf-Consistency\n6\n85.3\nMulti-Agent Debate (round 2)\n9\n83.0\nSelf-Consistency\n9\n88.2\nTable 8: Results of Constrained Generation.\n# calls\nCommonGen-Hard\nStandard Prompting*\n1\n44.0*\nSelf-Correct*\n7\n67.0*\nStandard Prompting*\n1\n53.0\nSelf-Correct*\n7\n61.1\nStandard Prompting (ours)\n1\n81.8\nSelf-Correct*\n7\n75.1\n* Prompts and results from Madaan et al. (2023).\n4\nMULTI-AGENT DEBATE DOES NOT OUTPERFORM SELF-CONSISTENCY\nAnother potential approach for LLMs to self-correct their reasoning involves allowing the models\nto critique and debate through multiple model calls (Du et al., 2023; Liang et al., 2023; Chen et al.,\n2023a). Du et al. (2023) implement a multi-agent debate method by leveraging multiple instances of\na single ChatGPT model and demonstrate significant improvements on reasoning tasks. We adopt\ntheir method to test performance on GSM8K. For an unbiased implementation, we use the exact\nsame prompt as Du et al. (2023) and replicate their experiment with the gpt-3.5-turbo-0301\nmodel, incorporating 3 agents and 2 rounds of debate. The only distinction is that, to reduce result\nvariance, we test on the complete test set of GSM8K, compared to their usage of 100 examples. For\nreference, we also report the results of self-consistency (Wang et al., 2022), which prompts models\nto generate multiple responses and performs majority voting to select the final answer.\nTable 7 presents the results. The results indicate that both multi-agent debate and self-consistency\nachieve significant improvements over standard prompting. However, when comparing multi-agent\ndebate to self-consistency, we observe that the performance of multi-agent is only slightly better than\nthat of self-consistency with the same number of agents (3 responses, the baseline also compared\nin Du et al. (2023)). Furthermore, for self-consistency with an equivalent number of responses,\nmulti-agent debate significantly underperforms simple self-consistency using majority voting.\nIn fact, rather than labeling the multi-agent debate as a form of “debate” or “critique”, it is more\nappropriate to perceive it as a means to achieve “consistency” across multiple model generations.\nFundamentally, its concept mirrors that of self-consistency; the distinction lies in the voting mech-\nanism, whether voting is model-driven or purely based on counts. The observed improvement is\nevidently not attributed to “self-correction”, but rather to “self-consistency”. If we aim to argue that\nLLMs can self-correct reasoning through multi-agent debate, it is preferable to exclude the effects\nof selection among multiple generations.\n5\nPROMPT DESIGN ISSUES IN SELF-CORRECTION EVALUATION\nIn Section 3, we observe that although self-correction decreases reasoning performance with all\ntypes of feedback prompts we have evaluated, performance varies with different feedback prompts.\nIn this section, we further emphasize the importance of proper prompt design in generating initial\nLLM responses to fairly measure the performance improvement achieved by self-correction. For\nexample, if a task requires that the model response should meet criteria that can be easily specified\n7\n\n\n--- PAGE 8 ---\nPublished as a conference paper at ICLR 2024\nin the initial instruction (e.g., the output should contain certain words, the generated code should\nbe efficient, the sentiment should be positive, etc.), instead of including such requirements only\nin the feedback prompt, an appropriate comparison would be to directly and explicitly incorporate\nthese requirements into the prompt for generating initial responses. Otherwise, when the instruction\nfor generating initial predictions is not informative enough, even if the performance improves, it\nis unclear whether the improvement merely comes from more detailed instructions in the feedback\nprompt or from the self-correction step itself.\nTo illustrate such prompt design issues in the self-correction evaluation of some prior work, we\ntake the Constrained Generation task in Madaan et al. (2023) as an example, where the task re-\nquires models to generate coherent sentences using all 20-30 input concepts. The original prompt in\nMadaan et al. (2023) (Figure 7) does not clearly specify that the LLM needs to include all concepts\nin the prompt; thus, they show that self-correction improves task performance by asking the model\nto identify missing concepts and then guiding it to incorporate these concepts through feedback.\nBased on this observation, we add the following instruction “Write a reasonable paragraph that\nincludes *ALL* of the above concepts” to the prompt for initial response generation (refer to Figure 8\nfor the full prompt). Following Madaan et al. (2023), we use concept coverage as the metric. We\nreference their results and replicate their experiments using gpt-3.5-turbo-0613. Table 8\ndemonstrates that our new prompt, denoted as Standard Prompting (ours), significantly outperforms\nthe results after self-correction of Madaan et al. (2023), and applying their self-correction prompt\non top of model responses from our stronger version of the standard prompting again leads to a\ndecrease in performance.\n6\nCONCLUSION AND DISCUSSION\nOur work shows that current LLMs struggle to self-correct their reasoning without external feed-\nback. This implies that expecting these models to inherently recognize and rectify their reasoning\nmistakes is overly optimistic so far. In light of these findings, it is imperative for the community to\napproach the concept of self-correction with a discerning perspective, acknowledging its potential\nand recognizing its boundaries. By doing so, we can better equip the self-correction technique to\naddress the limitations of LLMs and develop the next generation of LLMs with enhanced capabili-\nties. In the following, we provide insights into scenarios where self-correction shows the potential\nstrengths and offer guidelines on the experimental design of future self-correction techniques to\nensure a fair comparison.\nLeveraging external feedback for correction. In this work, we demonstrate that current LLMs\ncannot improve their reasoning performance through intrinsic self-correction. Therefore, when valid\nexternal feedback is available, it is beneficial to leverage it properly to enhance model performance.\nFor example, Chen et al. (2023b) show that LLMs can significantly improve their code generation\nperformance through self-debugging by including code execution results in the feedback prompt to\nfix issues in the predicted code. In particular, when the problem description clearly specifies the in-\ntended code execution behavior, e.g., with unit tests, the code executor serves as the perfect verifier\nto judge the correctness of predicted programs, while the error messages also provide informative\nfeedback that guides the LLMs to improve their responses. Gou et al. (2023) demonstrate that LLMs\ncan more effectively verify and correct their responses when interacting with various external tools\nsuch as search engines and calculators. Cobbe et al. (2021); Lightman et al. (2023); Wang et al.\n(2023b) train a verifier or a critique model on a high-quality dataset to verify or refine LLM outputs,\nwhich can be used to provide feedback for correcting prediction errors. Besides automatically gen-\nerated external feedback, we also often provide feedback ourselves when interacting with LLMs,\nguiding them to produce the content we desire. Designing techniques that enable LLMs to interact\nwith the external environment and learn from different kinds of available feedback is a promising\ndirection for future work.\nEvaluating self-correction against baselines with comparable inference costs. By design, self-\ncorrection requires additional LLM calls, thereby increasing the costs for encoding and generating\nextra tokens. Section 4 demonstrates that the performance of asking the LLM to produce a final\nresponse based on multiple previous responses, such as with the multi-agent debate approach, is in-\nferior to that of self-consistency (Wang et al., 2022) with the same number of responses. Regarding\n8\n\n\n--- PAGE 9 ---\nPublished as a conference paper at ICLR 2024\nthis, we encourage future work proposing new self-correction methods to always include an in-depth\ninference cost analysis to substantiate claims of performance improvement. Moreover, strong base-\nlines that leverage multiple model responses, like self-consistency, should be used for comparison.\nAn implication for future work is to develop models with a higher probability of decoding the opti-\nmal solution in their answer distributions, possibly through some alignment techniques. This would\nenable the model to generate better responses without necessitating multiple generations.\nPutting equal efforts into prompt design. As discussed in Section 5, to gain a better understanding\nof the improvements achieved by self-correction, it is important to include a complete task descrip-\ntion in the prompt for generating initial responses, rather than leaving part of the task description for\nthe feedback prompt. Broadly speaking, equal effort should be invested in designing the prompts\nfor initial response generation and for self-correction; otherwise, the results could be misleading.\n7\nLIMITATIONS AND BROADER IMPACT\nAlthough we have conducted a comprehensive evaluation spanning a variety of self-correction strate-\ngies, prompts, and benchmarks, our work focuses on evaluating reasoning of LLMs. Thus, it is\nplausible that there exist self-correction strategies that could enhance LLM performance in other\ndomains. For example, prior works have demonstrated the successful usage of self-correction that\naligns model responses with specific preferences, such as altering the style of responses or enhanc-\ning their safety (Bai et al., 2022; Ganguli et al., 2023; Madaan et al., 2023). A key distinction arises\nin the capability of LLMs to accurately assess their responses in relation to the given tasks. For\nexample, LLMs can properly evaluate whether a response is inappropriate (Ganguli et al., 2023),\nbut they may struggle to identify errors in their reasoning.\nFurthermore, several prior works have already shown that LLM self-correction performance be-\ncomes significantly weaker without access to external feedback (Gou et al., 2023; Zhou et al., 2023a)\nand can be easily biased by misleading feedback (Wang et al., 2023a), which is consistent with our\nfindings in this work. However, we still identified prevailing ambiguity in the wider community.\nSome existing literature may inadvertently contribute to this confusion, either by relegating crucial\ndetails about label usage to less prominent sections or by failing to clarify that their designed self-\ncorrection strategies actually incorporate external feedback. Regarding this, our paper serves as a\ncall to action, urging researchers to approach this domain with a discerning and critical perspective.\nWe also encourage future research to explore approaches that can genuinely enhance reasoning.\nREPRODUCIBILITY STATEMENT\nOur experiments utilize GPT-3.5 and GPT-4, which are accessible via the public API at https:\n//platform.openai.com/docs/models, as well as Llama-2, an open-source model. To\nfacilitate reproducibility, we detail the specific kernels used, e.g., gpt-3.5-turbo-0613, or\nprovide the access times for each experiment. We use prompts from previous works when possible.\nFor our designed prompts, we include the exact prompts in Appendix A.\nACKNOWLEDGEMENT\nWe would like to thank Chen Liang, William Cohen, Uri Alon, and other colleagues at Google\nDeepMind for valuable discussion and feedback.\nREFERENCES\nHussam Alkaissi and Samy I McFarlane.
